  snippet of section 29 the +0x1c call is ParseIniFile; the +0x2c call there is the
  same ReceiveMessage.) Section 25's other claim stands: +0x34 = AddSon.

*** SCRIPT SNOOP: a built-in call trace (very useful, previously unknown) ***
Two Lua globals registered next to Script_DoFile (section 32):
    Script_SnoopOn()   -> 0x4915e0 : mov dword [0x668a5c], 1 ; ret
    Script_SnoopOff()  -> 0x4915f0 : mov dword [0x668a5c], 0 ; ret
When the flag at 0x668a5c is non-zero, the engine logs EVERY dispatched call:
    "[S2E] %s: "   (script -> engine)  emitted in LUAF_Interface @0x490b89
    "[T2E] %s: "   (thread -> engine)  emitted at 0x48de4e and 0x48ecc8
  where %s is the TARGET NODE's name (fetched via node GetName 0x4ce7a0), followed by
  the command name and its marshalled arguments. Output goes through the normal log
  path, i.e. it lands in log.txt when started with -logfile -logunknown.

  PRACTICAL: putting Script_SnoopOn() at the top of a level script gives a complete,
  ordered trace of which script commands actually execute, against which node -- the
  single best debugging tool we have found for "is my callback running at all?".
  (Touchpoint, not worked on here: this is exactly the instrument the separate
  intro-radio investigation asked for -- it would show immediately whether the
  DelayedFunction/timer path ever reaches Game_SendRadioMessageTake, and on which
  node. Combine with the "[TIME] Function ... will be executed" line from
  C_ScriptTimer, section 35.)

  Note: 0x6676c8 is a separate log-verbosity counter (written at 0x41338d/0x4135ce/
  0x41384d); LUAF_Interface checks it before printing the "command has been sended to
  a NULL node" warning -- i.e. that warning is suppressed unless logging is on. This
  is why -logunknown was needed to see our sp_1n1 errors at all (section 6b-1).

STILL TODO: NOD_Message field semantics beyond +0x8/+0x10; message-id -> PM_ handler
routing inside ReceiveMessage; std_vessel.des; text-table format; DMUS_ music.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
37. MESSAGE ROUTING -- how a node executes a message (id -> handler), SOLVED
    (analysis session 2 cont.; GOG Aqua.exe)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Followed the message into the receiver. NOD_Directory's vtable (0x5f7178) slot +0x2c
is a thunk 0x46c530 -> the real handler 0x4414e0, which is the NOD_Node-level
message handler shared by every node class:

    0x4414e0:  esi = message
               eax = [esi + 8]            ; <<< msg[+8] IS THE MESSAGE ID
               eax += 0xffffec78          ; = eax - 5000  (subtract the Node_ block base)
               cmp eax, 0xd ; ja default
               jmp [eax*4 + 0x441888]     ; per-command jump table (14 entries)

  So message dispatch = "subtract my subsystem's block base, index a jump table".
  That is exactly WHY the ids are allocated in decimal 1000-blocks (section 27): each
  class handles one contiguous block, and the block base is a compile-time constant
  baked into the subtraction. Confirms msg[+8] = id (set from prototype->[+8], the
  value NOD_MessagePrototype::ctor(id) stored -- section 26/30/36).

  RESOLVED Node_ handler table @0x441888 (index = id - 5000):
      5000 Node_SetName          -> 0x441509      5007 Node_Find         -> 0x441668
      5001 Node_ParseIniFile     -> 0x44154c      5008 Node_GetPath      -> 0x4416cb
      5002 Node_EnterSimulation  -> 0x4417d3      5009 Node_GetNumOfSons -> 0x441738
      5003 (unused slot)         -> 0x4417e7      5010 Node_SubSon       -> 0x441750
      5004 Node_AddSon           -> 0x441597      5011 Node_GetSon       -> 0x441763
      5005 Node_CreateNode       -> 0x4415cb      5012 Node_GetSonName   -> 0x44178d
      5006 Node_GetName          -> 0x441534      5013 Node_GetTime      -> 0x4417ba
  (Note id 5003 has no registered prototype -- a cut/reserved Node_ command; its slot
  points at the default/ignore path. Same trick can find other cut commands: any block
  index whose jump-table slot exists but has no prototype.)

  Node_SetName's handler (0x441509) shows the shape: `edi += 0x10` (the node's name
  field at object+0x10), call 0x4be190 (find/lookup), then a virtual call [vt+4] --
  i.e. handlers read args out of the message and act on `this`.

GENERALISATION: to find ANY command's implementation, take its id (section 27 /
aquanox1_engine_classes.json), find the owning class's message handler (vtable +0x2c,
following the thunk), read the `add eax, -<blockbase>` constant to confirm the block,
then index that function's jump table at (id - blockbase). This is the mechanical
recipe that section 22's dead-end was groping for -- it works because dispatch is a
switch, not a per-function pointer.

WHY THE PM_ MIRROR EXISTS: since a call is a self-contained (id + args) message
object, the Process Manager can serialise it, send it to clients, and each client's
node runs the same jump table -- identical semantics locally and remotely. Sections
15/26/27/30/36/37 now form one complete, evidence-backed chain from Lua source line
to node-side execution.

CAVEAT: NOD_Node's own class record gave vtable 0x5f34bc in my factory scan, whose
+0x2c is COH_Stream_Reiceiver::SetType -- i.e. that scan picked the wrong vtable for
that record (multiple/inherited vtables). Trust the per-class thunk (NOD_Directory ->
0x46c530 -> 0x4414e0) rather than a blind +0x2c read on any vtable.

STILL TODO: dump the remaining per-class handler tables (Body 1000, Game 11000,
Vessel 15000, ...) to map all 308 commands to impl addresses; NOD_Message field
semantics beyond +0x8; std_vessel.des; text-table format; DMUS_ music.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
38. HANDLER TABLES PER BASE CLASS + the -logunknown machinery (partial result)
    (analysis session 2 cont.; GOG Aqua.exe)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Extended section 37 from Node_ to the whole scene-graph. Method that works: the
class vtable is the LAST `mov [reg], <0x5fxxxx>` inside the CTOR (the factory only
mallocs then calls/jmps to the ctor; the base ctor writes its own vtable first, the
derived ctor overwrites it -- so take the last one, not the first). Then read slot
+0x2c, follow the thunk, and read `add eax, -<blockbase>` + `jmp [eax*4 + <table>]`.

RESULT -- handler tables are PER BASE CLASS, shared by every derived class:
  * All NOD_Node-derived classes  -> block base 5000, table 0x441888 (14 slots)
      NOD_Directory, NOD_Scenario, NOD_Group, NOD_Director, NOD_Position, NOD_Path,
      NOD_PatrolArea, NOD_Hud, NOD_FX_Sky/Sound/Stream/Spawn/Infiltrate
  * All NOD_Body-derived classes  -> block base 1000, table 0x467994
      NOD_Body, NOD_ShieldedBody, NOD_Bullet, NOD_Buzzer, NOD_EscapePod, NOD_Flare,
      NOD_Fungus, NOD_Gun, NOD_Gun_Part, NOD_NPC_Gun, NOD_TorpedoLauncher,
      NOD_BuzzerLauncher, NOD_Halo, NOD_Light, NOD_Trail, NOD_ParticleEmitter,
      NOD_FX_Dipol/Light/Plankton/Rays/Seeder/Shockwave
  (35 scene classes resolved this way.)
So a nod_vessel does NOT own a Vessel_ jump table of its own next to the Body one --
each class handles ITS OWN id block and forwards everything else. Which brings us to:

THE DEFAULT PATH = the message-forwarding + unknown-command logger (0x4417f8):
    ecx = this->[+8]                    ; the parent/next handler
    if ecx: esi = call 0x537160(this, msg)     ; forward the message up the chain
    if still unhandled:
        eax = call 0x4c81a0( msg->[+8] )      ; LOOK UP THE PROTOTYPE BY MESSAGE ID
        if [0x6676c8] > 0:                    ; the log-verbosity gate (section 36)
            call [prototype_vt + 0xc]         ; GetName()
            ... print the unhandled command + the node ...
  => THIS IS THE -logunknown MACHINERY. It explains the "LOG UNKNOWN BEGIN/END"
  block and the "[NOD_Generic]: '/Scenario_Static/Object/...'" lines we saw in our
  own log.txt: those are messages that reached a node whose class does not handle
  that id block, dumped with the node's path. Useful, not an error per se.
  Also gives us: 0x4c81a0 = GetPrototypeById(id), 0x537160 = forward-to-parent.

HONEST PARTIAL: the Node_ table (0x441888) is DENSE and fully resolved (section 37).
The Body_ table (0x467994) is SPARSE -- reading 46 consecutive dwords runs off the
end into NOP padding (0x90909090) and code bytes. MSVC emits sparse switches as a
BYTE index table (`movzx eax, byte [eax + idxtbl]` before `jmp [eax*4 + tbl]`), so
the Body/Game/Vessel blocks need that second table decoded first. Not done yet --
do NOT trust a dense read of those tables. (Node_-style dense blocks are safe.)

CUT-COMMAND DETECTOR (works today): any slot in a DENSE table whose (blockbase+index)
has no registered prototype is a cut/reserved command. In the Node_ block that is
exactly one: id 5003 -> handler 0x4417e7 (the ignore path).

STILL TODO: decode the byte-index tables for the sparse blocks (Body 1000, Game
11000, Vessel 15000, ...) to map all 308 commands to impl addresses; NOD_Message
fields beyond +0x8; std_vessel.des; text-table format; DMUS_ music.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
38. HANDLER TABLES + a SELF-CORRECTION on "cut commands"
    (analysis session 2 cont.; applying and then stress-testing the section-37 recipe)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Section 37 proved the dispatch mechanism. Applying it at scale mapped 228 of 306
named commands to engine implementation addresses -> aquanox1_message_handlers.json.
But stress-testing the result exposed TWO of my own errors. Both are recorded here
because either one silently produces wrong data.

ERROR 1 -- "no two-level switches exist". WRONG. Several handlers use the classic
MSVC two-level switch. Verified on NOD_Body (handler 0x4675f0):
    eax = [edi+8]            ; message id
    add eax, 0xfffffc18      ; -1000  (Body block base)
    cmp eax, 0x2d ; ja 0x467980            ; 0x467980 = DEFAULT
    mov cl, byte ptr [eax + 0x4679d4]      ; 46-byte INDEX table
    jmp dword ptr [ecx*4 + 0x467994]       ; only 16 jump targets
So the "slot count" (46) is the INDEX-table length, while the jump table holds just
16 distinct handlers. Reading the jump table with 46 entries walks off its end into
code bytes (0x90909090 = nops) -- which is exactly what produced garbage "handler"
addresses. ALWAYS bound a jump table by "entries that are valid .text addresses".

ERROR 2 -- "13 cut/reserved commands". Mostly WRONG. Unregistered ids usually point
at the DEFAULT handler, which is normal for a sparse switch and means nothing.
Decoding Body's index table properly: ids 1017, 1027, 1028, 1029, 1031, 1032, 1042
all carry index 15 = 0x467980 = the default -> NOT cut commands, just holes.
After proper verification only TWO unregistered ids have their OWN handler code:

  *** VERIFIED CUT COMMANDS (own handler, no script prototype) ***
   Body id 1034 -> handler 0x46792b : `push edi ; mov ecx,esi ; call 0x467bd0`
        Sits between Body_EMPParalyzed (1033) and Body_AddItemInteger (1035), i.e.
        in the item/status region of the Body block.
   Node id 5003 -> handler 0x4417e7 : `mov edx,[edi] ; mov ecx,edi ; call [edx+0x28]`
        A virtual call on the node itself (vtable slot +0x28), between
        Node_EnterSimulation (5002) and Node_AddSon (5004) -- plausibly the
        counterpart "Node_LeaveSimulation".  (Note: Node_LeaveSimulation IS used by
        campaign scripts per section 14, yet has no prototype id of its own here --
        worth re-checking; it may be this very command, exposed under another name,
        or a Lua-side helper.)
  Both are real engine code with no Lua binding. Disassembling 0x467bd0 / the vtable
  slot +0x28 target would say what they do.

METHOD THAT WORKS (class-agnostic sweep, used for the 228):
  1) find every `jmp [reg*4 + imm32]` in .text (542 sites)
  2) capstone-decode the 80 bytes before; collect sub/add/lea immediates as blockbase
     candidates and cmp immediates as slot counts
  3) accept the (base,count) whose slots resolve >=50% to known message ids
  4) dump the table -- BUT bound it by valid .text addresses, and if a `movzx
     byte ptr [reg + imm]` precedes the jmp, resolve the two-level indirection first.
Blocks recovered: 1000 Body, 2000 Script, 3000 ParticleEmitter, 5000 Node, 11000 Game,
12000 Light, 19000 Position, 20000 Trigger, 23013 PhysicalCharacter, 24000 PatrolArea,
25007 Turret, 32001/37000/39001/39002 ANT, 33000 ShieldedBody, 35000 WayPoint,
43000 Gun, 15042 Vessel. Handlers often split one block across consecutive
sub-switches (bases 1014, 1038, 12001, 15042, 23013) -- all inside their decimal
1000-block, confirming section 27 again.

REMAINING GAP (honest): 78 commands unmapped (Vessel 20, Vehicle 14, Emitter 6,
Stream 5, PhysicalCharacter 4, Player 4, ...). Also: the ParticleEmitter/WayPoint
"cut" ids from the earlier draft were NOT verified and are withdrawn -- they must be
re-checked with the two-level decoding above before any claim is made.

STILL TODO: re-verify cut ids for ParticleEmitter/WayPoint with correct table bounds;
disassemble 0x467bd0 and node vtable+0x28; the remaining 78 handlers; NOD_Message
fields beyond +0x8; std_vessel.des; text-table format; DMUS_ music.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
39. CORRECTION -- there are 398 prototypes, not 308; only 2 real cut commands
    (analysis session 2 cont.; a significant self-correction)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
This section REPLACES my earlier "24 cut commands" claim and corrects the prototype
COUNT used since section 26.

THE ROOT ERROR. Section 26's class dump used a push-counting parser over the 471
RegisterClass call-sites. That parser silently dropped ~90 classes (their args are
passed partly via mov/registers, not push), so it reported 308 message prototypes.
That undercount then made ordinary commands look "unregistered" in sections 38/39.

THE RELIABLE COUNT. Every script command has a GetName getter (the B8<str>C3 stub at
vtable+0xc, section 28). There are 398 such getters = 398 prototypes. Cross-linking
each getter's vtable to its factory's `push id; call 0x4c80a0` gives 325 with a
resolved message id. New deliverable aquanox1_message_ids.json (id -> name, 398 total).
Corrected per-subsystem prototype counts: Vessel 56, ANT 48, Body 46, Game 35,
Vehicle 28, PhysicalCharacter 23, Camera 21, Turret 17, Trigger 16, Node 14,
WayPoint/Player 11, ShieldedBody 8, ParticleEmitter/Emitter 7/6, PatrolArea/Light 6,
Script/Gun/Stream 5, Position/ItemBox 4, Mine/Console 3, ...

CONSEQUENCE FOR "CUT COMMANDS". Re-checking my 23 flagged ids against the full 398
list: 21 were FALSE POSITIVES -- they DO have a prototype my 308-list lacked:
   5003 Node_LeaveSimulation; 7000 Camera_SetBackPlane, 7005 Camera_LookAt,
   7006 Camera_RollTo, 7010 Camera_ShakeHit, 7011 Camera_ShakeTo, 7012 Camera_ZoomTo,
   7015 Camera_MoveToAnglesAbs, 7016 Camera_Cut, 7020 Camera_SwingAtLissajous;
   11000 Game_LoadScript, 11006 Game_LoadFog, 11025 Game_LoadTextTable;
   15050 Vessel_PseudoExplode, 15054 Vessel_DeactivateTurningInEmp;
   23022 PhysicalCharacter_SetHitPropability; and 5 ANT_ network messages.
So the "9 cut camera commands" were NOT cut -- the camera actually has 21 prototypes,
MORE than I thought (incl. the nice finds Camera_SwingAtLissajous, MoveToAnglesAbs).
The Node_LeaveSimulation "mystery" from section 38 is likewise resolved: id 5003 IS
Node_LeaveSimulation, exactly as its position (between EnterSimulation and AddSon)
suggested.

WHAT ACTUALLY REMAINS CUT (own handler, no prototype in the full 398 list):
   Body 1017 -> 0x54c7f4  (shield-info-cluster query; writes a return flag, uses the
       Request*Info list helper 0x4ba6e0) -- an unexposed kin-shield query.
   Body 1034 -> 0x46792b -> 0x467bd0  (item/status setter variant).
Both are internal Body counterparts, not gameplay content. Net: essentially NO
cut script commands -- the earlier cut-command excitement was an artifact of the
undercount. (This does NOT affect the cut-content ASSET findings of section 13/29,
which came from PAK scans, e.g. the phobocaster team ship.)

LESSON (kept deliberately): a derived "master list" that is silently incomplete
turns every gap into a false discovery. The getter-based list (398) is now the
authoritative id/name source; prefer aquanox1_message_ids.json over the older 308
figures wherever they conflict.

STILL TODO (revised): re-run the section-38 handler sweep against the 398 list to see
true remaining coverage; disassemble Body 1017/1034; resolve remaining blocks; NOD_
Message fields; std_vessel.des; text-table format; DMUS_ music.

40. THE MESSAGE-ID REGISTRY, FIXED PROPERLY -- 390/398 ids (supersedes 27 & 39)
    (analysis session 2 cont.; closing the counting mess)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Section 39 corrected the prototype COUNT (398) but still only had 325 ids resolved,
which made section-38 handler sweeps produce bogus "cut" slots (registered Body ids
looked empty). Root cause now fixed.

WHERE THE ID REALLY LIVES. Each prototype's FACTORY sets it:
    NOD_Body_SetCS factory 0x4c29a0:
        push 0x10 ; call malloc ; push 0x3ec (=1004) ; call 0x4c80a0
        (NOD_MessagePrototype::ctor(id)) ; mov [esi], 0x5f957c (its vtable)
My earlier vtable->id pass keyed on the wrong vtable for many classes (the class has
several; the factory's final `mov [esi],vt` is the authoritative one). Parsing the id
as "the push immediately before call 0x4c80a0" IN THE FACTORY, and reading the name
from that factory's vtable+0xc getter, resolves it correctly.

RESULT: 390 of 398 prototypes now have a correct id (was 325). Deliverable
aquanox1_message_ids.json rewritten (by_id and by_name). Spot-checks that were WRONG
before and are right now: Body_SetPosition=1000, Body_SetCS=1004,
Body_SetKineticShield=1008, Camera_Cut=7016, Node_LeaveSimulation=5003. Corrected
subsystem sizes: Vessel 56, ANT 48, Body 38 (Body's block also holds ~8 non-Body
message ids interleaved), Game 35, Vehicle 28, PhysicalCharacter 23, Camera 21.

IMPACT ON EARLIER SECTIONS:
  * Section 27's "308 ids, decimal 1000-blocks" -> the BLOCK SCHEME is confirmed and
    unchanged, but the COUNT is 398 prototypes / 390 ided. Use this file, not the 308.
  * Section 38/39 handler coverage numbers were computed against the incomplete id
    map; aquanox1_message_handlers.json is now re-validated against the 390 registry
    (each handler carries confirmed_name). The honest mapped/total is ~140/390 -- the
    matcher still misses if/else-style handlers (Vessel/Vehicle/Turret/etc.), a tool
    limit, not a model gap.
  * The "cut command" question is effectively CLOSED: with the correct registry there
    are no confirmed cut script commands beyond the two internal Body ids (1017, 1034),
    and even those await 100% id resolution of the Body block to be certain. Treat cut-
    COMMAND claims as unproven; the cut-CONTENT ASSET findings (sections 13/29) are
    separate and stand.

LESSON (second time, so it is now a rule): do not compute "unregistered => cut" from a
derived id map unless that map is provably complete. Always resolve ids at the factory
(push before call 0x4c80a0) and confirm the name via the factory's own vtable getter.

STILL TODO: resolve the last 8 ids; extend the handler matcher to if/else handlers;
NOD_Message fields beyond +0x8; std_vessel.des; text-table format; DMUS_ music.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
41. THE NOD_Message OBJECT (408 bytes) -- what a script call is packaged as
    (analysis session 2 cont.; dispatcher 0x48ff30 @ 0x490090)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Decoded the message the dispatcher builds for every script call (malloc 0x198 =
408 bytes, at 0x490090):
    msg = malloc(0x198)
    msg[+0]    = 0x5f43e8        ; vtable (NOD_Message)
    msg[+8]    = prototype->[+8] ; THE MESSAGE ID  (getter 0x5efc40 = mov eax,[ecx+8];
                                  ; ebp = the prototype, so this copies its id field)
    msg[+0xc]  = 0              ; (result/return slot, cleared)
    msg[+0x10] = 0xFD           ; byte flag  (constant 253 -- message "kind"/priority?)
    msg[+0x11] = 1              ; byte flag  (1 = ? "active"/"local")
    msg[+0x12] = 0 (word)       ; cleared
    msg[+0x14] = 0 (word)       ; cleared  (arg count filled as args are appended)
Then base-ctor 0x43e560 runs (links it into the RTI/list system like every node).
So +8 is the id the receiver switches on (section 37: `[esi+8] - blockbase`), and
+0x10/+0x11 are small fixed flags -- consistent with a fixed message header followed
by a variable argument area (the remaining ~0x180 bytes hold the marshalled args,
written by the arg loop via the slot allocator 0x4543c0, section 31).

ARG LOOP CONTROL (same function): a stack counter [esp+0x1c] starts at 0 and runs to
GetArgCount; for each i it calls `[prototype+0x18]` = GetParamType(i) (vtable slot,
section 28), `cmp eax,0xd ; ja` then the type switch (section 31). [esp+0x14]=1 marks
"has at least one arg". After the loop the [S2E]/[T2E] trace may print (section 36),
then delivery: `mov edx,[node] ; call [edx+0x2c]` = NOD_Node::ReceiveMessage(msg)
(section 36/37).

WHY 408 BYTES. A generous fixed buffer so any command's argument set (up to a dozen
ints/floats/vectors/strings/node-handles) fits inline without a second allocation --
important because these messages are created every frame for every AI action and,
for MP, serialised and sent. The fixed header (+8 id, +0x10/+0x11 flags) is exactly
what a network layer needs to route a serialised message; this is the concrete shape
of the "a script call is a network-replicable message" story (sections 15/26/37).

NOD_Message class: vtable 0x5f43e8; it is a registered class (RTI), base of the
message system alongside NOD_MessagePrototype (section 26). The prototype is the
STATIC descriptor (name/arity/param types + id); NOD_Message is the RUNTIME instance
(id + actual arg values) that travels to the node and (optionally) across the wire.

STILL TODO: exact meaning of the +0x10=0xFD / +0x11=1 flags (compare a networked vs
local message); the arg-area layout written by 0x4543c0; extend handler matcher to
if/else handlers; std_vessel.des; text-table format; DMUS_ music.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
42. THE TAKE / TEXT SYSTEM (mtake_*_d.des) -- textkey -> text + voice + speaker
    (analysis session 2 cont.; PAK data)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Decoded how a numeric "take key" (used by Game_SendRadioMessageTake / WayPoint_
SetTakeKey_* / [textkey] in OSDs) resolves to on-screen text AND a voice sample.

FILES: dat/sty/mtake_<level>_d.des (one per level, 43 files) + mtake_gen_d.des (80
generic, level-independent) + mtake_mp_d.des + mtake_dogfight/asylum_d.des. Loaded
via Game_LoadMTake("...") at level start. 734 takes total, 619 with a voice sample.

TAKE RECORD (plain-text DES):
    [TakeN] {
        Key   = 29                       // the number scripts pass around
        Person= 90                       // speaker -> person_d.des (90 = "Sally")
        Text  = "Unser Hobby-Terrorist..."// the HUD/subtitle string
        Wav   = "d\mtake\c1\k1m05d0029"// voice sample path (under sfx/sample/)
        Mood  = 11                        // -> mood_d.des (portrait expression)
    }
So ONE key ties together: display text + voice file + speaker portrait + mood. The
Wav path maps to sfx/sample/<Wav>.* (the c1/c2 dirs = chapter voice banks; this is
exactly where the custom intro test put ns_op_intro_narrow, section: funk handoff).

KEY-SPACE (global, non-overlapping):
    campaign takes: 1 .. ~1367  (each level owns a scattered set; gen_ = 703..1265)
    instant-fight/custom: 9000+  (dogfight 9000-9002, asylum 9100, and the custom
        radio test used 9200/9201)
    MP: 5001..5006
person_d.des: Key -> Name/ShortName + portrait TGAs (ImageElf = the HUD comm
portrait, hud_elf_characterNN.tga) + Sex. mood_d.des: 24 mood entries (portrait
expression variants). So SendRadioMessageTake(key) -> look up take -> show Person's
ImageElf with Mood, print Text, play Wav.

*** TOUCHPOINT (noted, not worked) *** for the intro-radio investigation: this
confirms the DATA side is straightforward and was proven working (custom key 9201
fired from a kill callback). It also means a start-of-level radio line needs BOTH a
loaded mtake (Game_LoadMTake) AND a valid Person entry for the portrait -- but the
blocker was never the data (a Person=0 take like dogfight 9000 "Folge den NAV-
Punkten" has no portrait/voice and still shows text). The blocker is the trigger
context/timing (sections 34/35). So for a text-only start message, a take with
Person=0 + empty Wav (like the dogfight NAV takes) is the minimal test payload once
Script_SnoopOn (section 36) proves the call fires.

STILL TODO: the [textkey] number in OSDs vs take Key (same namespace?); stake_d.des
(345 KB = the station dialogue trees); the +0x10/+0x11 NOD_Message flags; if/else
handler matcher; std_vessel.des; DMUS_ music.

43. THE KEY NAMESPACES -- textkey, cargo, pilot names, takes (they are SEPARATE)
    (analysis session 2 cont.; PAK data)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
An OSD's [textkey] block does NOT use the take keyspace of section 42. Each numeric
key field indexes its OWN plain-text .des table:

  gen_toiler~.osd [textkey]:  name = 278 , cargo0 = 1110 , cargo1 = 1119 , cargo2 = 1003
       name   -> dat/sty/pilot_d.des    (278 = "NA-Toiler")
       cargoN -> dat/sty/cargo_d.des    (1003 = "Sulfide")

  MAP OF KEY NAMESPACES:
    pilot_d.des     160 entries, keys 1..340 -- TWO ranges in one file:
        1..~101   random PILOT NAMES (Arkcroth, Druid, Darklander, NiTo, Perseus,
                  Chrissi, Sebbl, SamSoNight, Creole ...). This is the pool that
                  Body_SetNameKey(-1) draws from -- the source of our "Jeff the
                  Ripper" wave enemies (section 6b-1).
        200..340  OBJECT/TYPE NAMES with faction prefixes:
                  ATL- (Atlantic Federation), NA- (Neopolis/player side), CRA-
                  (Crawler), ENT- (EnTrOx), BNT- (Biont).
                  e.g. 271 NA-Avenger, 273 NA-Phobocaster, 278 NA-Toiler,
                  258 ENT-Sprungschiff, 263 ENT-Synapsenstation, 267 ENT-Turbine,
                  252 ATL-Brainfire, 256 ATL-NavyHQ, 260 ATL-Parlament,
                  250 NA-Mine, 269 NA-Torpedoturm, 282 "???" (a deliberate unknown).
        => this is the HUD target label ("what am I looking at").
    cargo_d.des     104 entries, keys 1001.. -- cargo/freight names (Manganknoll.,
        Methanhydrat, Sulfide, Titan...). A ship OSD lists up to 3 cargo keys.
    nod_item_d.des   50 entries, keys 1.. -- in-world item names (DoomMortar,
        EMPactor, Hitman, LaserGatling) as displayed on pickups.
    mtake_*_d.des   734 takes, keys 1..1367 / 9000+ / 5001..5006 (section 42).
    Plus the display DBs of section 12: ship_d, gun_d, item_d, mission_d, briefing_d,
    person_d (speakers), mood_d, acknowledge_d (64 short confirmations), room_d,
    station_d, stake_d (345 KB station dialogue).

CONSEQUENCE FOR MODDING: numbers in .osd/.sco are meaningless without knowing WHICH
table they index. A ship's [textkey].name is a pilot_d key; its cargo0..2 are cargo_d
keys; a radio message key is an mtake key; a pickup's label is a nod_item_d key. When
adding content, allocate new keys in the right file (all are plain text, easy to edit)
and keep them inside that file's range to avoid collisions.

NICE FIND: pilot_d.des's faction prefixes (NA-/ATL-/CRA-/ENT-/BNT-) independently
confirm the faction mapping we derived from AN2 (section: AN2 factions) -- EnTrOx and
Bionts already had first-class HUD labels in AN1 even though EnTrOx content was largely
cut (section 13). Entry 282 = "???" is the engine's placeholder for an unidentified
target.

STILL TODO: stake_d.des (station dialogue trees, 345 KB); room_d/station_d; the

44. STATIONS + THE CUT STATION "Habitat daGama" (roadmap-relevant)
    (analysis session 2 cont.; PAK data: station_d.des, stake_d.des, sta_*.sco)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
THE STATION SYSTEM. dat/sty/station_d.des holds 25 station records:
    Key, Name, Territory, Depth, Logo/Image (menu TGAs), BGSound,
    EngineScript = "map\<level>\script\sta_<name>"      <-- stations ARE levels
So docking at a station loads a real .sco scene (its own map folder), the same engine
path as a mission -- e.g. Entropoint II -> map\1n2\script\sta_entropointII.sco.
Several stations SHARE a script (Lima 1 & Lima 2 -> sta_lima1; Gotham -> sta_entroxhq;
Deepdraft 1 & Jules 1 -> sta_deepdraft1), i.e. the same interior is re-dressed.

STATION DIALOGUE. dat/sty/stake_d.des = 1269 "station takes", identical record shape
to the mission takes of section 42 (Key/Person/Text/Wav/Mood) but with Wav paths
d\stake\c<chapter>\k<ch>d<dialog>t<take> -> 211 DIALOGUES across 6 chapters
(ch1 224 takes, ch2 181, ch3 266, ch4 188, ch5 209, ch6 172). Longest dialogue: 19
takes. Speakers (person_d.des): Emerald Flint 535 lines (the player), Lt. Boston
Harper 50, Commodore Sool 40, Iwan King 30, Sobinoso Chaka 30, Sergej Chang 27,
Cpt. Eliza deGrange 26 ("Credits" 29 = the credits crawl reuses the take system).
room_d.des: 91 rooms (Dock, Haendler, ...) each with Station key, Category, a menu
background TGA and a room ambience .sam.

*** CUT CONTENT: sta_dagama.sco *** map/1n1/script/sta_dagama.sco EXISTS in the PAKs
(46,756 bytes, 115 protos -- a FULL station scene: atl_dock3, atl_wb3a_* wall blocks,
16+ gen_contain_* containers) but NO station in station_d.des references it. "Habitat
daGama" survives only in TEXT: briefing_d.des, mtake_1n1_d.des (the drunk architecture
student "bemaengelt die konstruktivistische Langeweile des Habitat daGama!") and
stake_d.des. => a station that was built, voiced-around, and then dropped from the
station table. Restoring it needs only a station_d.des record pointing EngineScript at
map\1n1\script\sta_dagama (+ a Logo/Image, and room_d entries if rooms are wanted).
This is a concrete, low-risk target for the roadmap item "cut content: sta_* stations".

MORE GAPS suggesting cuts: station keys jump 13 -> 19 (14..18 missing) and 24..29,
31..39 are absent; Territory values present are 1, 2, 4, 6, 11 -- TERRITORIES 3 and 5
have no stations at all. Whether those were ever populated is unproven from the data
alone, but the numbering is suggestive.

45. STATION DIALOGUE BINDING (sdialog~.des) + what sta_*.sco scripts really are
    (analysis session 2 cont.; PAK data)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
DIALOGUE BINDING. dat/sty/sdialog~.des is COOKED (descook it). It holds 211 dialogues:
    [dialogN] { key, type, name, station, room, numoftake, take0..takeN }
    type: "story" 93 / "substory" 118
    name: a DEVELOPER LABEL with chapter-sequence numbering, e.g.
        "1-20 Begruessung Perry", "1-30 El Topo Begruessung", "1-40 Sool Begruessung",
        "1-50 Harper: brauchst du uns?", "1-80 Neru plaudert ueber Bionten"
    station/room: where it plays (station_d.des / room_d.des keys)
    takeN: indices into stake_d.des (section 44)
So a dialogue = an ordered list of takes, pinned to a station+room, tagged story or
substory. Dialogue density: Neopolis 40, Atacama City 37, Scavenger 22,
Speicherkraftwerk 19, Topo's Asylum 14, Magellan 13. Room 130 hosts 41 dialogues (a
generic "intercom/common" room). NOTE: every dialogue's station key exists in
station_d.des -- so the cut station daGama (section 44) had NO dialogues written.

WHAT sta_*.sco ACTUALLY IS. Comparing the 16 station scripts shows they are NOT
interiors (interiors are the 2D .usi/menu GUI, as in AN2). They are the EXTERIOR
approach/backdrop scenes -- the 3D world you see around the station:
    sta_entropointii  26 KB,  71 objects: 19 fungus, 9 generic, EnTrOx ships
                      (ent_bomber/jumpship/jumpstar/scout), green plankton
    sta_eltopo        56 KB, 149 objects: 77 Positions, 13 Paths, 6 PatrolAreas,
                      elt_asylum.osd, atl_dock3, cyan plankton, sky+rays
    sta_neopolis      67 KB, 167 objects: 84 Positions, 14 vessels (atl_fregatte,
                      atl_ec_triumph, atl_scout1), several atl_dock1_* variants
i.e. docks + ambient traffic + flora + atmosphere. Positions/Paths/PatrolAreas drive
the idle traffic circling the station.

THE CUT STATION, CONFIRMED FROM TWO SIDES. sta_dagama.sco (46,756 B, 87 objects) fits
this pattern exactly: atl_dock3, atl_wblock3a_05/_build/_build_a wall blocks, corals,
fungus, cyan plankton, rays, 5 ambient vessels, 2 patrol areas, fog map/1N1/Fog/fog.fog,
ambient light (0.02, 0.09, 0.14), and it loads mtake_gen_d.des.
  * Its wall blocks are the *_build variants = a station UNDER CONSTRUCTION.
  * The SAME build meshes (atl_wblock3a_build, _build_a, _05) appear in the mission
    level 1n1's mesh list -- so mission 1N1 is fought AT Habitat daGama, and the drunk
    architecture student in mtake_1n1 take 29 is literally complaining about the
    building he is floating next to.
  * The station scene exists and is complete; only the station_d.des record (and any
    dialogues) were dropped. It reuses 1N1's fog, so it was authored alongside that map.
=> Restoring: add a station_d.des row {Key=<free, e.g. 14>, Name="Habitat daGama",
   Territory=1, Depth=<~2800>, Logo/Image=<reuse an existing TGA>, BGSound=
   music_ambient.sam, EngineScript="map\1n1\script\sta_dagama"}. Rooms/dialogues are
   optional -- with none, it is a dockable backdrop. Lowest-risk cut-content restore
   we have found.

wall blocks. sta_dagama is the dockable twin of the 1N1 battle scene: mission 1N1 is
fought outside Habitat daGama, and this script is what you would have seen when docking
there.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
47. THE ENEMY MATRIX IN THE ENGINE -- storage, evaluation, and the E/F/N/U values
    (analysis session 2 cont.; GOG Aqua.exe; completes/settles section 18)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Traced the friend-or-foe matrix from the script commands into the engine.

STORAGE. The matrix lives on the Game object: [0x66323c] (the Game singleton) + 0xaf4
= a 16x16 byte array (256 bytes). Confirmed by all three command handlers:
    Game_SetEnemyMatrixElement (id 11017, handler 0x45557d) -> Matrix::Set 0x440ef0:
        if row<0x10 and col<0x10:  matrix[col*16 + row] = value(byte)   (ret 0xc)
    Game_GetEnemyMatrixElement (id 11018, handler 0x4555d8) -> Matrix::Get 0x440ec0:
        return matrix[col*16 + row]
    Game_ResetEnemyMatrix     (id 11019, handler 0x455639) -> Matrix::Fill 0x440f20:
        rep stosd 0x40 dwords -> fills all 256 bytes with the default value.
    ResetEnemyMatrix pushes 2 as the fill value => the DEFAULT cell value is 2.
So: 16 teams (0..15, the Body_SetFriendOrFoeID ids of section 18), one byte per
ordered pair, indexed matrix[col*16+row]. row = the asking object's team, col = the
other object's team (they are set per-direction, matching section 18).

THE VALUE ENCODING (settled from the radar-colour switch at 0x466d63, jump table
0x466e80 indexed by value-1):
    1 = FRIEND   -> radar GREEN  (sets G byte, R=B=0)
    2 = NEUTRAL  -> radar BLUE   (sets B byte)  <-- the reset default
    3 = UNKNOWN  -> special path (call 0x43e670), rendered distinctly
    4 = ENEMY    -> radar RED    (sets R byte)
(So the script constants E/F/N/U of section 18 map to bytes 4/1/2/3. Section 18
counted N as the huge default -- consistent: reset fills everything with 2=Neutral,
and levels then poke in the few 4=Enemy / 1=Friend pairs they need.)

WHERE THE AI READS IT. Matrix::Get 0x440ec0 has 9 callers; the important ones:
  * TARGET SELECTION (0x43618f and 0x436a0e, inside an enemy-scan loop): for each
    candidate it does
        push [candidate + 0x54]     ; candidate's team id  (col)
        push [self + 0x2c]          ; own team id          (row)
        mov ecx, [self + 0x9c]      ; the matrix object
        call 0x440ec0               ; = matrix[row][col]
        cmp al, [edi] ; jge skip    ; compare against a threshold, skip if not hostile
    => the AI walks nearby objects, looks up the matrix cell, and keeps only those
    whose value passes the hostility test. This is the gate before Vessel_Attack /
    the BHV_ layer (sections 15/17): the matrix decides the target set.
  * RADAR/HUD COLOURING (0x46447b: `cmp al,3` special-case; 0x466d63: the 4-way
    colour switch above) -- paints each contact by its matrix value.
  * Two more in the netcode region (0x5ed607/0x5ed672) = MP replication of relations.

OBJECT FIELD OFFSETS discovered here (useful for any body-poking mod):
    body + 0x2c  = own FriendOrFoe team id (row)
    body + 0x54  = (as seen on candidates) team id used as col
    body + 0x9c  = pointer to the matrix/Game context the AI queries
    body + 0xac  = a sub-object (dereferenced before reading +0x54 in radar paths)
    Game + 0xaf4 = the 256-byte matrix; Game singleton ptr at 0x66323c.

MODDING UPSHOT: our sp_1n1 matrix pokes (matrix[2][4]=E etc., section 18) write byte
4 into Game+0xaf4 at [4*16+2]; the toilers' FriendOrFoeID 4 and player 2 are the row/
col. Everything we did from script is exactly this engine path. To make a faction
mod, ResetEnemyMatrix then Set the pairs you want; radar colour follows automatically
(1 green / 2 blue / 3 special / 4 red).

STILL TODO: the exact hostility threshold [edi] in the target loop (is "enemy" value
>= some cutoff, or == 4?); the +0x10/+0x11 NOD_Message flags; if/else handler
matcher; std_vessel.des; DMUS_ music.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
47. THE DEATH / DEBRIS SYSTEM (vfx/dying/std_*.des + EXP_N fireworks)
    (analysis session 2 cont.; closes the section-16 [dying] TODO)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
An object's .osd [dying].dyingdata names one of 29 DEATH PROFILES in vfx/dying/
(cooked DES -> descook). Schema:
    [data]
      minimaldyingtime / maximaldyingtime   seconds the death animation lasts
      firework      = "EXP_<n>"             which explosion to play (see below)
      tumblefactor  = 0..1                  how wildly the wreck tumbles
      acceleration  = float                 buoyancy/drift while dying
                                            (NEGATIVE for turrets/vehicles: -30, -40
                                             = they sink instead of drifting up)
      [corps]
        numofspawners   = N                 how many debris emitters spawn
        distancerelation                    debris spread
        [sonpropability] prob0..prob4       which debris piece each spawner picks
                                            (0.33/0.33/0.34 = uniform over 3 types)

THE 29 PROFILES (dyingtime / firework / tumble / accel / spawners):
    std_vessel            2.0  EXP_4  0.5  4.0  2      <- the default fighter
    std_vessel_sml        2.0  EXP_5  1.0  4.0  1
    std_vessel_bomber     4.0  EXP_6  0.5  2.0  4
    std_vessel_frigate    8.0  EXP_7  0.5  1.0  8
    std_vessel_frigate_big / _big2   8.0  EXP_10/EXP_12  0.5  1.0  8
    std_vessel_manycorpses 8.0 EXP_12 0.5  1.0  111   (!! a wreck that showers debris)
    std_vessel_ghost      19.0 EXP_16 0.4  0.3  8     (longest, slowest death)
    std_vessel_loyd      10.0  EXP_25 0.2  2.0  2
    std_vessel_magmaeater 12.0 EXP_11 0.5  0.5
    std_vessel_bio / _bomber_bio     EXP_8 / EXP_9    (Biont deaths)
    std_vessel_oldone / _oldone_head EXP_17 / EXP_18  (the "Old One")
    std_player            2.0  EXP_4  0.5  4.0        (player dies like a std vessel)
    std_player_bomber     4.0  EXP_6
    std_turret 1.0 EXP_1 accel -30 ; std_turret_big 1.0 EXP_2 -30 ; std_vehicle 1.0 EXP_3 -40
    std_generic 2.0 EXP_14 ; std_trash 0.2 EXP_13 ; std_turbine 2.0 EXP_15
    std_generic_brainfire/_ec_tower/_parlament/_geb_hab_big  12.0  EXP_21..EXP_24
        (big structures burn for 12 s with their own fireworks)
    std_character 4.0 EXP_19 ; _marax EXP_19 ; _crocell 4.0 EXP_20 1.0 1.0

THE FIREWORK TABLE. The exe holds two parallel string arrays in .data, both in
DESCENDING index order:
    EXP_1..EXP_30            @ 0x607d94 downward, 8-byte stride
    "osd/fx_explosion/fx_exp_dying1..30.osd"  @ 0x605299 downward, 36-byte stride
=> the mapping is positional: firework "EXP_N" plays vfx/osd/fx_explosion/
   fx_exp_dyingN~.osd. Each of those is a normal OSD (particle emitters, flashes,
   shockwave, debris children) -- the same [child] machinery as ships (section 16).
Supporting assets: vfx/osd/debris/ deb_small1/2, exp_burn01/02, exp_cloud01,
exp_flash01, exp_foam01, exp_meteor01, exp_sparkle01.

*** UNUSED CONTENT *** The exe knows 30 fireworks and all 30 fx_exp_dying OSDs ship,
but the 29 death profiles only ever reference EXP_1..EXP_25. EXP_26..EXP_30
(fx_exp_dying26..30) are complete, loadable explosions that nothing in the game uses.
A one-line edit to any std_*.des (firework = "EXP_28") would bring them back --
though they are cooked, so rebuild with descook. Low-risk, purely cosmetic cut content.

MODDING VALUE: death behaviour is 100% data. To make an enemy explode more
spectacularly, point its .osd [dying].dyingdata at std_vessel_frigate (8 spawners) or
even std_vessel_manycorpses (111), or swap its firework. No code involved.

STILL TODO: room_d Category semantics; the +0x10/+0x11 NOD_Message flags; if/else
handler matcher; DMUS_ dynamic music.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
48. THE MUSIC / AUDIO SYSTEM -- .sam descriptors, mood fragments, DirectMusic
    (analysis session 2 cont.; GOG Aqua.exe + PAK data; closes the DMUS_ TODO)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
SCRIPT SIDE (ids from the corrected registry, section 40):
    Game_LoadMusic  id 11029  handler 0x4553f2 -> NOD_Game::LoadMusic 0x45a040
    Game_PlayMusic  id 11009  handler 0x4553e1 -> NOD_Game::PlayMusic 0x458ea0
  LoadMusic parses a name + a SLOT INDEX and bounds-checks it (`cmp [esp+0xc], 5`)
  -> at most 5 music slots. PlayMusic resolves the target by NAME and special-cases
  the string "atmo" (@0x608c24) -- the ambient/atmosphere slot. So a level loads up
  to five tracks into slots and plays them by slot/name; PM_LoadMusic / PM_PlayMusic
  mirror both for MP (section 15).

FILE FORMAT. Every sound is a PAIR:
    <name>.mp3    the actual audio
    <name>~.sam   a COOKED DES descriptor (descook it) with one [sampleinfo] block:
        name    = "music\music_track01.mp3"   (path relative to sfx/sample/)
        freq    = "44ac"  (0x44ac = 17580? no -- it is the raw 44100 Hz tag as text)
        vol     = 250     (0..255)
        freqrnd / volrnd  random jitter on pitch/volume (empty = none)
        cnt / minr / maxr repetition count and min/max radius (for positional sfx)
  So scripts and OSDs always reference the .sam; the engine reads the mp3 path from
  it. Same descriptor type is used for room ambience (sfx/sample/room/dock.sam,
  section 44) and for engine loops in ship OSDs (section 16 [sound][engine]).

MUSIC LIBRARY (67 files: 30 mp3 + 37 sam):
    music_track01 .. music_track10   the score
    MOOD FRAGMENTS -- short cues layered/switched by mood:
        track01_frag_heroic1, _heroic2, _tension
        track02_frag_trance
        track07_frag_heroic1, _frag_sad3      (track07 exists ONLY as fragments)
        track08_sad
    STINGERS / SFX BEDS: music_sfx_cataclysm2, _evilmachine, _futurealarm,
        _matrixdist2, _mystery2, _spacetrip, _timesend
    SITUATIONAL: music_ambient (stations), music_badend, music_fightclub_atmo,
        music_fightclub_won, music_fightclub_badend
  The "frag_<mood>" naming plus the 5-slot loader is AN's dynamic-music scheme:
  a base track in one slot, mood fragments in others, switched by game state. Only
  tracks 01, 02 and 07 ever got fragments -- the system is richer than the content.

DirectMusic: the exe links the full DMUS_ error enum (DMUS_E_*, dozens of strings)
and DirectMusic script errors -- so DirectMusic/DirectSound is the backend, but the
GAME's own layer is the .sam + slot scheme above. There is also an fmod reference
(AN2 ships fmod.dll; AN1's exe merely mentions it).

MODDING NOTES (relevant to our "music in Narrow" mod, section 6):
  * To add a track: drop <name>.mp3 into sfx/sample/music/ AND a <name>~.sam whose
    [sampleinfo].name points at it. Without the .sam the engine cannot find the mp3.
  * Game_LoadMusic(node, "<sam-name>", slot) then Game_PlayMusic(node, slot) --
    slot < 5. Reusing an existing name (e.g. music_fightclub_atmo) is the safe path
    and is what our Narrow mod does.
  * music_track07 has no full version -- if a mod references "music_track07" it will
    fail; use music_track07_frag_heroic1 instead.

STILL TODO: room_d Category semantics; the +0x10/+0x11 NOD_Message flags; if/else
handler matcher; disassemble Body 1017/1034 (the two unexposed commands).

49. REGISTRY COMPLETE (398/398) -- and there are NO cut script commands
    (analysis session 2 cont.; final word on sections 38/39/40)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Closed the last 8 unresolved ids and, with them, the whole "cut command" thread.

THE TELL. The corrected registry (section 40) had 390/398 ids and left exactly 8 gaps
in the Body block: 1017, 1027, 1028, 1029, 1031, 1032, 1034, 1042. Independently, the
campaign scripts use exactly 8 Body_ functions that had no id: Body_RequestKinInfo,
Body_RejectEnemies, Body_EnemyArrival, Body_InformEnemyPatrol, Body_EnemyArrivalPatrol,
Body_SetFriendOrFoeID, Body_AddItem, Body_StartEscapeBehaviour. Eight gaps, eight
orphaned names -- and the Body block's own symmetry predicted the pairing
(1017 must be RequestKinInfo, because Emp/Bio use Request/Reject/BarrierReached at
1020-1022 / 1023-1025 while Kin only had Reject 1018 + Barrier 1019).

RESOLVED (widened the backward window when reading `push id` before the base-ctor call;
these 8 factories place the push further from the call than the rest):
    1017 Body_RequestKinInfo        1029 Body_InformEnemyPatrol
    1027 Body_RejectEnemies         1031 Body_EnemyArrivalPatrol
    1028 Body_EnemyArrival          1032 Body_SetFriendOrFoeID
    1034 Body_AddItem               1042 Body_StartEscapeBehaviour
Note how they sit exactly where you would expect: AddItem right before
AddItemInteger (1035); StartEscapeBehaviour right before StopEscapeBehaviour (1043);
SetFriendOrFoeID inside the enemy cluster. aquanox1_message_ids.json now holds
398/398 ids, no duplicates -- it is the authoritative name<->id source.

*** THE CUT-COMMAND QUESTION IS CLOSED: THERE ARE NONE. ***
Every id with its own handler in every decoded block maps to a registered script
command. The earlier "13", then "24", then "2" cut commands were all artifacts of an
incomplete id map. What remains true and useful from that hunt:
  * a jump table must be bounded by "entries that are valid .text addresses";
  * two-level MSVC switches (movzx cl,[reg+idxtbl] ; jmp [ecx*4+jmptbl]) DO exist,
    e.g. NOD_Body's handler 0x4675f0 (46-entry index table -> 16 targets);
  * unregistered slots normally point at the block DEFAULT (Body's is 0x467980);
  * a "switch" found by pattern scan can be a false positive: the one at 0x41ff26
    (base "1048", 121 slots) is not a message handler at all.
Cut CONTENT (assets: sta_dagama, phobocaster team ship, EnTrOx meshes, EXP_26..30
explosions) is a separate, still-valid line of findings -- see sections 13, 29, 44-47.

METHOD NOTE (for future id work): read the id at the prototype's FACTORY as the last
`push <imm>` before the base-ctor call, scanning back up to ~46 bytes, and take the
name from that factory's vtable+0xc getter. Both the vtable and the window size matter.

STILL TODO: room_d Category semantics; the +0x10/+0x11 NOD_Message flags; extend the
