--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
50. HANDLER MAP COMPLETE (391/398) -- the MSVC "base-folded jump table" trick
    (analysis session 2 cont.; supersedes the 228/306 sweep of section 38)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Mapped 391 of 398 script commands to their engine implementation address. The
earlier sweeps stalled at ~228 because they assumed the handler does
`sub id, blockbase ; jmp [id*4 + table]`. Most handlers DON'T subtract at all -- the
compiler FOLDS the base into the table address:
    jmp dword ptr [eax*4 + K]         where K = table_start - 4*blockbase
So handler(id) = *(K + id*4) directly, with NO explicit sub/cmp using the base. That
is why searching for `sub reg, 15000` etc. found nothing for Vessel/Vehicle/Turret.

THE METHOD THAT WORKS (final): for each of the 542 `jmp [reg*4 + K]` sites, treat K as
a base-folded table and test whether *(K + id*4) lands in .text for the ids of one
subsystem; accept when >=60% of a subsystem's ids resolve. This one idea lifted
coverage 228 -> 369 -> 391. Deliverable aquanox1_message_handlers.json rewritten:
{name: {message_id, handler, subsystem}} for 391 commands, plus method/coverage/
unmapped. Subsystems now fully or near-fully mapped incl. Vessel, Vehicle, Turret,
PhysicalCharacter, Player, ShieldedBody, ANT, Light, Stream, Emitter, ParticleEmitter,
PatrolArea, Gun, Script.

REMAINING 7 (trivial/attach handlers whose table the matcher didn't latch onto):
    FX_Plant_SetMap, Path_GetPositionOrientation, Position_SetPosition,
    Position_SetRadius, WayPoint_Attach, WayPoint_InformActivated,
    WayPoint_InformDeactivated. Not a model gap -- their blocks are tiny (Position has
    4 ids, WayPoint's attach/inform are singletons) so the ">=60% of a subsystem"
    acceptance rule rejects them; they can be read by hand from their class's table.

WHAT THIS GIVES US. Every meaningful script command now has: name <-> message id
(section 49, aquanox1_message_ids.json) <-> engine handler address
(aquanox1_message_handlers.json). To learn exactly what any API call does to the
engine, disassemble its handler. E.g. the combat/shield commands (Body 1005-1025),
the AI verbs (Vessel 15000+), the camera moves (Camera 7000+) are all now addressable.

CROSS-CHECK. Handlers for the same subsystem cluster in one code region (Vessel around
0x586508, Vehicle around 0x501c88, Turret around 0x4cc6f8, ANT split across several),
matching the class-per-file layout and confirming the base-folded tables belong to the
right NOD_ classes.

STILL TODO: room_d Category semantics; the +0x10/+0x11 NOD_Message flags; read the 7
trivial handlers by hand; (deep) walk a few high-value handlers (e.g. the damage loop
Body_*ShieldBarrierReached) to document exact behaviour.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
51. THE SHIELD OBJECT LAYOUT (in-memory) -- per-face banks, confirmed from code
    (analysis session 2 cont.; disassembled the Body shield handlers)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Disassembled the shield get/set handlers to recover the runtime shield struct, which
backs the .osd [shield] data of section 16.

  Body_GetKineticShield handler 0x4677e1:
      call [body_vtable + 0x70]        ; get the shield sub-object pointer
      test eax,eax ; je -> return -1.0 (0xbf800000)   ; no shield object -> "-1"
      mov cl, [face] ; cmp cl, 6 ; jge -> return -1.0  ; FACE INDEX must be 0..5
      -> six faces (right/left/front/back/top/bottom, matching the OSD in/inmax set)
      call 0x467b10 (GetKineticFace) ; return the value
  GetKineticFace 0x467b10 (this is the whole function):
      fild  dword ptr [ecx + face*4 + 0x24]   ; CURRENT shield value for that face
      fidiv dword ptr [ecx + face*4 + 0x4c]   ; divided by that face's MAXIMUM
      ret                                     ; returns a FRACTION 0..1
  => in the shield sub-object: +0x24 = current[6] (int per face), +0x4c = max[6]
     (int per face). Getter returns current/max, so scripts see a 0..1 fraction; -1
     means "this face has no shield" -- the exact -1 sentinel used in .osd.
  Body_SetEmpShield path 0x467b90 (mirror for EMP, reads max at +0x68):
      eax = [ecx + 0x68] (the EMP max) ; if < 0 skip (unshielded);
      current = round(max * fraction) ; store to [ecx + 0x40]
  So the three damage types have their own current/max scalars at fixed offsets
  (kinetic per-face arrays at +0x24/+0x4c; emp/bio totals near +0x40/+0x64/+0x68),
  reachable through the body's vtable slot +0x70 (GetShieldObject).

DATA SIDE cross-check (strings in the exe): the shield/armor field vocabulary is
  ArmorKIN, ArmorEMP (ship_d.des armor totals), showshield, DamagePropagationRatio,
  kinetic/emp/bio in/inmax per face (OSD, section 16). Weapons: damage1..damage15
  (a bullet/weapon can carry up to 15 damage entries) and armor25/armor100 mesh tags.

  ShieldedBody_ commands (section 15) and their handlers:
    MakeVulnerable 33001 (0x5a173e) / MakeInvulnerable 33000 (0x4b44d0) /
    MakeSelectiveVulnerableAgainst 33002 (0x498d00) -- toggle which damage types can
       hurt a face (used by scripted invulnerability, e.g. story bosses);
    CoupleFather 33007 (0x431104) -- chains a child body's shield to its parent
       (turrets on a ship share the hull's shield state);
    DeepDestruct 33006, SetLogarithmicMode 33004/33005 -- destruction + a log-scaled
       damage response mode.

CLOSES THE LOOP with section 17 (the damage vector) and 16 (the OSD [shield]): the
projectile's out-vector (kin/emp/bio) is subtracted from the target face's current[]
array here; when current hits 0 the KinShieldBarrierReached message (id 1019) fires
back to the script (section 14 callbacks). HONEST LIMIT: the exact subtract-and-clamp
site (the code that writes current[face] on a hit) sits inside the collision/damage
path and was not pinned to a single instruction this session -- the STRUCT and the
getters are confirmed; the write-on-hit is inferred from the barrier-reached callback
and the field layout, not yet disassembled.

STILL TODO: pin the on-hit subtract site (start from the projectile [shield].out reader
and the KinShieldBarrierReached 1019 emitter); room_d Category; the +0x10/+0x11
NOD_Message flags; the 7 trivial handlers.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
51. THE SHIELD DATA STRUCTURE + shield API internals (disassembled)
    (analysis session 2 cont.; using the section-50 handler map)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Walked the Body_ shield handlers into the engine. The ShieldedBody's shield object
(reached from a Body via virtual call [vtable+0x70] = GetShieldedBody) stores, per
the SIX faces (right/left/front/back/top/bottom, indices 0..5), TWO parallel int
arrays:
    shield + 0x24 + face*4   = CURRENT shield points (int)      <- what damage subtracts
    shield + 0x4c + face*4   = MAXIMUM shield points (int)      <- the .osd inmax value
So one shield bank = 6 currents at +0x24.. and 6 maxes at +0x4c.., 0x28 bytes apart.
(There are three such banks per ship -- kinetic/emp/bio -- laid out consecutively;
this is the +0x24/+0x4c pair for one bank.)

THE ACCESSORS (exact math):
    GetShieldValue(face) @0x467b10:
        return (float)current[face] / (float)max[face]      ; fild/fidiv -> NORMALISED
        => Body_GetKineticShield returns 0.0..1.0, NOT absolute points.
    SetShieldValue(value, face) @0x467b20:
        current[face] = round( value * max[face] )           ; value is 0..1, scaled
        (uses the classic +(-0.4999995) trick 0xbefffffc for round-to-nearest)
        => Body_SetKineticShield takes a FRACTION 0..1, not points.

THE SCRIPT WRAPPERS (from the handlers, section 50):
    Body_GetKineticShield 0x4677e1: reads face byte arg; if face >= 6 -> return -1.0
        (0xbf800000); else return GetShieldValue(face). So -1 = "invalid face".
    Body_SetKineticShield 0x46776d: face byte; if face == 0xFF (-1) it loops i=0..5
        calling SetShieldValue(value,i) = set ALL faces at once; else the one face.
        (Same 0xFF="all faces" convention as the SetNameKey(-1) idiom elsewhere.)
    Emp/Bio variants call the same helpers on the emp/bio bank.

MODDING CONSEQUENCE (important, corrects a natural assumption):
  Body_SetKineticShield(node, fraction, face) expects a FRACTION 0..1 of the face's
  max (from the .osd [shield].inmax, section 16), NOT an absolute point value, and
  face 0..5 or -1 for all faces. Body_GetKineticShield returns that fraction (or -1
  for a bad face). To "recharge shields to full" you pass 1.0; to knock a face to
  half, 0.5. This is why the glue.sco helpers are named SetStateValue_KineticShield25
  /50 (section: 1n1 strings) -- they set fractions.

CALLBACK vs COMMAND (a clean confirmation of section 19's callback protocol):
  Body_InformShotHit (id 1007) and Body_KinShieldBarrierReached (id 1019) both map to
  the block DEFAULT handler 0x467980 -- i.e. the engine does NOT act on them when
  received. They exist so SCRIPTS can define them; the engine CALLS them (engine->
  script) when a shot lands / a shield bank breaks. Their being "no-op on receive" is
  the signature of an engine->script callback, distinct from a real command.

DAMAGE LOOP (where it lives): projectile impact does not go through these script
setters; it reduces current[face] directly from the projectile's [shield].out damage
vector (section 17) inside the collision/shot-hit path, then fires the *ShieldBarrier
Reached / InformShotHit callbacks when a bank crosses zero. The setters above are the
SCRIPT-facing view of the same arrays.

STILL TODO: the exact per-face current[]-= damage step in the collision handler; the
three-bank stride (kinetic vs emp vs bio base offset); room_d Category; NOD_Message
+0x10/+0x11 flags; the 7 trivial handlers.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
52. MESSAGE HANDLER INHERITANCE -- "default" means "forward to the base class"
    (analysis session 2 cont.; corrects a wrong reading in section 51)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
CORRECTION to section 51. I read handlers that point at the block "default" address
as "no-op engine->script callbacks". That is WRONG for many of them. The default is
not a no-op: it FORWARDS the message to the base class's handler. Message dispatch
mirrors the C++ class hierarchy.

THE CHAIN (verified for the Body block):
    NOD_Body handler 0x4675f0 : switch on (id-1000); its DEFAULT (0x467980) does
        call 0x4414e0
    0x4414e0 = NOD_Node handler : switch on (id-5000); its default (0x4417f8) does
        call 0x537160
    0x537160 : switch on (id-39002) = the ANT_Message block
So an id not handled by NOD_Body falls through to NOD_Node, then onward. A command is
executed by whichever class in the chain OWNS that id's block. (The classes: a
concrete node is e.g. NOD_Vessel -> NOD_ShieldedBody -> NOD_Body -> NOD_Node, each
adding its own id block; the handler walks up this chain via the default arm.)

CONSEQUENCE: "handler == block default" does NOT prove a command is a callback. It
only means THIS class doesn't own that id; a base class might. E.g.
Body_SetFriendOrFoeID (1032) shows the Body default, but it is a real command handled
higher/lower in the chain. To find a command's TRUE implementation you must follow the
default-forward chain until a switch arm other than the default catches its id.

WHAT IS ACTUALLY A CALLBACK (revised test): an id is an engine->script callback only
if NO class in its chain has a non-default arm for it -- i.e. the message falls all
the way through to the root and is dropped. The clean structural signals remain:
  * the *Request*/*Reject*/*Inform*/*Arrival*/*BarrierReached* naming (the AI/shield
    negotiation protocol, section 19) -- these ARE script-defined callbacks;
  * Body_ObjectDestroyed / EscapePodEjected / EMPParalyzed -- past-tense "it happened"
    notifications the engine sends up to the script.
But the FORWARD ones -- SetMesh, ActivateShieldGuard, SetFriendOrFoeID, Activate/
DeactivateEscapePod, Set*EscapeBarrier -- are real commands owned by a base class, NOT
callbacks. My section-51 Body split (15 command / 31 callback) OVERCOUNTED callbacks;
the true command set is larger because base-class-owned ids were miscounted.

DELIVERABLE IMPACT: aquanox1_message_handlers.json lists, per command, the handler of
its OWNING class where the sweep found a non-default arm. Where it shows a block
default address, read it as "resolved by a base class" and follow the chain
Body(1000) -> Node(5000) -> ... The 391/398 coverage stands; the interpretation of
"default" is what section 52 fixes.

VERIFIED ADDRESSES for the chain: NOD_Body 0x4675f0 (block 1000, default 0x467980 ->
0x4414e0); NOD_Node 0x4414e0 (block 5000, jump table 0x441888, default 0x4417f8 ->
0x537160); 0x537160 (block 39002 = ANT). NOD_ShieldedBody sits between Body and Node
for shield ids.

ALSO NOTED: Body_AddItem (id 1034, handler 0x467bd0) is a STUB -- it only logs
"WARNING: body '%s' received a Body_AddItem('%s', %d)" and does nothing. Items are
added via Body_AddItemInteger (1035) / ItemBox_. The extra shield setters at
[shield+0x40]/[shield+0x48] (0x467b50/0x467b90) use the same round trick as
SetShieldValue and set single scalar shield attributes.

STILL TODO: trace one command (e.g. Body_SetFriendOrFoeID) down to its owning class
arm to demonstrate the chain end-to-end; room_d Category; NOD_Message +0x10/+0x11.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
53. HANDLER MAP CORRECTED -- the 391 figure was over-eager; 131 verified
    (analysis session 2 cont.; a self-correction on section 50)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
While tracing Body_SetFriendOrFoeID down the inheritance chain (section 52) I found
that MANY jump tables map id 1032 -- and any other id -- onto *some* .text address.
That exposed a flaw in section 50's method: accepting a base-folded table K whenever
K+id*4 lands on ANY code pointer is far too weak. Spot-checks proved it: the 391-map
had Vessel_Goto -> 0x42ee10 (a string-copy loop), Player_FirstWarning -> unrelated
code, etc. So the 391 number was WRONG for a large fraction of entries.

FIX. aquanox1_message_handlers.json rebuilt with a strict, plausibility-checked
method: for each `jmp [reg*4+imm]`, DECODE the actual switch (base, jump table,
optional two-level index table, default, count); accept only if
  (a) >=3 of the block's ids are known commands of ONE subsystem (no foreign ids),
  (b) the resolved handler arms lie NEAR the jmp (<=0x8000) -- i.e. they belong to
      the same ReceiveMessage function, not to random code the folded index happened
      to hit.
Result: 131 STRICTLY VERIFIED handlers (Game 34, ANT 29, Body 23, Camera 21, Node 14,
Vessel 5, PhysicalCharacter 5). Spot-checks now sane: Body_SetPosition 0x46761c,
Camera_MoveTo 0x444af9, Game_SetAmbientLight 0x455775.

The remaining ~267 are NOT proven wrong -- they are simply not strictly verified; their
switches use shapes the conservative filter rejects. The RELIABLE way to get any of
them is by hand from its class's ReceiveMessage switch, exactly as done for
NOD_Body 0x4675f0 (two-level: 46-entry index table 0x4679d4 -> 16 targets) and
NOD_Node 0x4414e0 (jump table 0x441888). Those two are fully verified.

LESSON (third time a derived shortcut misfired -- now a firm rule): a positional/
folded table lookup will "resolve" for almost any index; ALWAYS validate that the
result (a) is contiguous within one subsystem and (b) is code adjacent to the switch.
The earlier 228 and 369/391 numbers should be treated as upper bounds, not facts;
131 is the verified floor. (This does not affect sections 49's id<->name registry,
which is exact, nor sections 51/52's shield/inheritance findings, which came from
hand-disassembled handlers.)

STILL TODO: harvest the remaining handlers per-class by hand (Camera/Game switch
entry points confirmed workable; Vessel/Vehicle/Turret next); room_d Category;
NOD_Message +0x10/+0x11.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
54. HANDLER MAP -- 141 handlers, double-verified (the reliable version)
    (analysis session 2 cont.; supersedes sections 50 and 53)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Rebuilt the handler map on a strong, principled signal and cross-checked it two ways.

THE RELIABLE SIGNAL. A real message handler must READ THE MESSAGE ID, which lives at
message+8 (section 41). So a genuine ReceiveMessage switch is preceded by
`mov reg, [obj+8]` within ~100 bytes of its `jmp [reg*4 + table]`. Requiring that
load (filter A) kills the base-folded false positives outright.

TWO INDEPENDENT FILTERS, ZERO CONFLICTS:
  A) [obj+8]-load present before the switch  -> 116 handlers
  B) contiguous single-subsystem block with arms near the jmp (section 53) -> 131
  Merged: 141 handlers. On the 106 names both filters produced, they agreed on the
  address in EVERY case (0 conflicts) -- strong evidence the addresses are correct.
  A final plausibility pass drops any arm that is string-copy/rep code.

RESULT: 141 verified handlers -- Game 34, ANT 29, Body 23, Camera 21, Node 14,
Trigger 10, PhysicalCharacter 5, Vessel 5. Spot-checks all sane:
    Body_SetPosition 0x46761c, Camera_MoveTo 0x444af9, Game_SetAmbientLight 0x455775,
    Trigger_SetPresenceSphere 0x54aee2 (mov [edx],1 = set the sphere flag).
aquanox1_message_handlers.json now carries {handler, message_id, subsystem} for these
141 plus method/coverage/warning.

WHY NOT MORE. Vessel/Vehicle/Turret/WayPoint/Light etc. dispatch without a nearby
[obj+8] load (the id is already in a register from a caller, or their switch sits
behind another thunk), so the conservative filters skip them. They are reachable by
hand from each class's ReceiveMessage (as done for NOD_Body 0x4675f0 two-level and
NOD_Node 0x4414e0). 141 is the trustworthy floor; do NOT resurrect the 391 figure.

NET STATE OF THE HANDLER THREAD (sections 37,38,50,53,54): the DISPATCH MECHANISM is
fully understood and proven (id at msg+8; per-class switch `id - blockbase`; default
arm forwards to the base class, section 52). The exhaustive id->address TABLE is
partially verified (141/398) and should be completed per-class by hand rather than by
any positional-table heuristic, which has now misfired three times.

STILL TODO: hand-harvest Vessel/Vehicle/Turret handlers from their class switches;
room_d Category; NOD_Message +0x10/+0x11 flags; the DMUS_ layer detail.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
55. HANDLER MAP -- final state 149/398, and where to stop
    (analysis session 2 cont.; closes the handler-address thread)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Extended the [obj+8]-verified sweep (section 54) to catch a few more real switches
(loosening the block-size floor to 2 while KEEPING the strict "pure single-subsystem
block + [obj+8] id-load" rule). Result: 149 verified handlers (Game 35, Body 30,
ANT 29, Camera 21, Node 14, Trigger 10, PhysicalCharacter 5, Vessel 5). All spot-checks
sane (Body_SetCS 0x4676db, Camera_Cut 0x444cdb, ...). aquanox1_message_handlers.json
updated to 149.

WHY THE REST RESISTS AUTOMATION -- and why that's fine. The AI subsystems (Vessel,
Vehicle, Turret, PhysicalCharacter) do not use one big switch. Their ReceiveMessage is
a CHAIN OF TINY SUB-SWITCHES, each handling ~7 consecutive ids, e.g. NOD_Vessel at
0x54b970:  mov edx,[eax+8]; add edx,0xffffc562 (= -15006); cmp edx,6; jmp [edx*4+
0x54b9e0] -- that arm owns only Vessel_Goto/GotoStop/GotoArrived/GotoPosReached/
GotoOverride/GotoFromPosition/FromPosition (15006-15012). Dozens of these little arms,
most interleaved with callback ids that go to a default, defeat a generic acceptance
rule (too few real commands per arm, too many defaults). They ARE all readable by hand:
walk the vessel vtable's message method, then each `add reg,-<firstid>; jmp [reg*4+tbl]`
arm in sequence. This is mechanical but not worth automating blindly (the automation
misfired 3x, sections 50/53/54).

DECISION: stop the exhaustive id->address harvest at 149 verified. What matters is
already secured:
  * the DISPATCH MECHANISM is fully proven and documented (sections 37/41/52): a script
    call becomes a NOD_Message (id at +8), sent to the node, whose ReceiveMessage
    switches on id-blockbase and forwards unknowns up the class chain;
  * name <-> id is EXACT for all 398 (section 49, aquanox1_message_ids.json);
  * 149 handler addresses are verified for the data-heavy subsystems a modder actually
    pokes at (Game world/atmosphere, Body physics+shields, Camera, Trigger, Node);
  * the per-arm recipe is written down for anyone who needs a specific AI handler.
For a 25-year-old game this is already a far more complete map of the script<->engine
bridge than ever existed publicly; further address harvesting is documentation polish,
not new understanding.

STILL OPEN (low priority): per-arm Vessel/Vehicle/Turret handler addresses; room_d
Category semantics; the NOD_Message +0x10/+0x11 flag meanings; DMUS_ layer detail.
These are noted for future sessions but are not blockers for any modding task.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
56. THE TRIGGER + WAYPOINT SYSTEM -- mission triggers, end to end (for scripting)
    (analysis session 3; API signatures + campaign bytecode + SensorLib/glue)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
This is the core "make something happen when X" machinery of every mission. Both
Trigger_ and WayPoint_ split into COMMANDS (you call them, they have handlers) and
CALLBACKS (the engine calls them on your bound state table).

TRIGGER_ API (block 20000):
  COMMANDS (configure the sensor volume):
    Trigger_SetPresenceSphere(radius)              20002  handler 0x54aee2  (most used)
    Trigger_SetBoxMode(dx,dy,dz)                   20015  handler 0x54b044  (a box volume)
    Trigger_SetVisibilityCone(dir:vec3, ang, len)  20003  handler 0x54af6a
    Trigger_SetVisibilityConeRelative(ang,len)     20004  Trigger_SetVisibilityBlockingStatus(b) 20005
    Trigger_SetAcousticSphere(radius)              20000  SetAcousticSensibility(s) 20001
    Trigger_ActivateSensor(a,b,c) 20006 / StopSensor() 20007  (library-driven; scripts
        never call these directly)
    Trigger_SetInterestList(s,s,s,s)               20008  (which node classes trip it)
  CALLBACKS (define these on the bound table; engine calls them):
    Trigger_EnterPresence / LeavePresence  (20009/20010)
    Trigger_EnterAcoustic / LeaveAcoustic  (20011/20012)
    Trigger_EnterVisible  / LeaveVisible   (20013/20014)

WAYPOINT_ API (block 35000):
    WayPoint_SetRadius(r)                           35000
    WayPoint_Attach(node)                           35006  (attach to a node/position)
    WayPoint_SetTakeKey_Enter/Inside/Leave/Outside/Activate  35004/35002/35005/35003/35001
        -> each binds an mtake KEY (section 42) fired when the player enters/is inside/
           leaves/is outside/activates the waypoint. THIS is how radio lines are pinned
           to places. (node, uint32 key) each.
    WayPoint_InformActivated/Deactivated/Entered/Left  35007-35010 = callbacks.

USAGE ACROSS THE CAMPAIGN (measured): WayPoints appear in ALL 31 mission levels, and
ALWAYS as the full set of 6 SetTakeKey_* + SetRadius + Attach together -- i.e. every
mission uses a fixed "waypoint with 5 take slots" idiom. Triggers appear in 20/25;
SetPresenceSphere (20x) and SetBoxMode (15x) dominate; visibility cones are rare (2x);
ActivateSensor/StopSensor are NEVER called directly (library only).

HOW A TRIGGER IS WIRED (from 1h1, verified end to end):
  1) BUILD (in __InitLayout): create the node, place it, size the volume:
        t = Node_CreateNode("NOD_Trigger", "BiontWakeUp")
        Node_AddSon(triggerDir, t)                 -- parent = .../Trigger directory
        Body_SetFriendOrFoeID(t, 3)                -- which team's members trip it
        Body_SetPosition(t, MAT_Vector3(909.35, 1815.92, 260))
        Trigger_SetPresenceSphere(t, 150)          -- 150-unit sphere
        Node_EnterSimulation(t)
  2) BIND (in __StartUp): give it a state table, exactly like any vessel:
        o3551 = BindEasy(Node_Find("/Scenario_Dynamic/Trigger"), "BiontWakeUp", S_o3551)
     Triggers live under /Scenario_Dynamic/Trigger (their own directory).
  3) REACT: the state table's sensor callback runs when something enters. The library
     helper SS_AvpStack (sensor.sco) is the canonical presence reaction:
        function SS_AvpStack(state) 
            SetEngineValue(state, "Triggered", 1)      -- raise a flag on the thread
            Node_LeaveSimulation(state.Node)           -- one-shot: disable itself
        end
     The mission then polls state.Triggered (or the sensor pre-empts the normal state
     function -- see below) to advance.

THE SENSOR-CALLBACK MECHANISM (ties into sections 34/35):
  glue.sco InsertSensorBehaviour(thread, key, fn):  thread.Sensors[key] = fn
  __GetStateFunction (section 34) checks G_SensorCallbackTable / thread.Sensors FIRST,
  before the normal State.CommandTable function. So a fired sensor PRE-EMPTS the state
  machine for that tick. SensorLib provides ready reactions: SS_AvpStack (presence ->
  set Triggered + self-disable), SS_ShotHit, SS_ShieldGuard, SS_EnemyGuard,
  SS_Threatening. StartEnemy/StartShieldGuard/StartShotHit (glue) arm them;
  Stop* disarm.

MINIMAL RECIPE TO SCRIPT A NEW TRIGGER (data + script, no exe):
  __InitLayout:  Node_CreateNode("NOD_Trigger", NAME); AddSon to the Trigger dir;
                 Body_SetPosition; Trigger_SetPresenceSphere(r) (or SetBoxMode);
                 optionally Body_SetFriendOrFoeID to filter who trips it;
                 Node_EnterSimulation.
  __StartUp:     BindEasy(Node_Find(".../Trigger"), NAME, S_state) where S_state has an
                 EnterPresence/Triggered handler (reuse SS_AvpStack or write your own
                 that calls Game_TextMessage / SendRadioMessageTake / spawns a wave).
  This is exactly the pattern our wave mod already uses for its PatrolArea; a Trigger is
  the same idea with a sensor volume instead.

TOUCHPOINT for the intro-radio problem: WayPoint_SetTakeKey_Enter(wp, key) is the
engine's OWN, timing-safe way to fire a radio take when the player reaches a spot --
no __StartUp/timer race (sections 35/36). A waypoint at the player's spawn with
SetTakeKey_Enter would speak the intro line the moment play begins, from a guaranteed-
live callback context. (Noted, not built here.)

STILL TODO: the exact args of Trigger_ActivateSensor(a,b,c) (which sensor kinds);
the AvpSensorComplex activation wrapper; decompile SS_ShieldGuard/SS_EnemyGuard;
WayPoint Inside/Outside semantics (continuous vs edge).
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
57. THE AI BEHAVIOUR LIBRARY (lib/*.sco) -- the ready-made mission building blocks
    (analysis session 3; decompiled all behaviour tables)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
This is what campaign missions are actually BUILT FROM. Instead of writing AI, a
mission binds a vessel to a pre-made BEHAVIOUR TABLE from these libraries. 27
behaviours across 8 files.

THE UNIVERSAL SHAPE. Every behaviour is a Lua table whose KEYS ARE ENGINE CALLBACK
NAMES (section 19's callback protocol) plus an activator and a stopper:
    LVA_GuardArea = {
        LVA_Activate            = fn,   -- runs when the behaviour starts (GotoState)
        Vessel_AttackOver       = fn,   -- engine fires this when a fight ends
        Body_EnemyArrivalPatrol = fn,   -- engine fires when an enemy enters the patrol
        StopBehavior            = fn,   -- teardown
    }
When bound as a thread's State (BindEasy / GotoState, sections 34/56), the engine
calls the matching key whenever that event occurs. So a behaviour = a small state
machine expressed as {event -> reaction}. Reactions call the real Vessel_/Body_
commands (Vessel_Patrol, Vessel_AttackPatrol, Vessel_AttackTurrets, StartEnemyPatrol).

PARAMETERS via GetInnerValue. A behaviour reads its tuning from the thread's "inner
values" (StoreInnerValue/GetInnerValue, glue.sco). Each behaviour has a PREFIXED
parameter set, e.g. LVA_GuardArea uses VGA_PatrolArea, VGA_Velocity, VGA_Mode,
VGA_Diameter, VGA_First..FourthPriority, VGA_TriggerArea, VGA_FollowArea. So to
configure a behaviour you StoreInnerValue(thread,"VGA_Velocity",v) etc. before
activating. (The First..FourthPriority values are the enemy-target preference order.)

THE CATALOGUE (behaviour : its event keys):
  vesselattack.sco -- area combat:
    LVA_ClearArea         Activate/AttackOver/EnemyArrivalPatrol/PatrolFinished/Stop
    LVA_GuardArea         Activate/AttackOver/EnemyArrivalPatrol/Stop   (patrol+defend)
    LVA_GotoAttack        + Vessel_GotoPosReached/GotoArrived (fly there, then attack)
    LVA_GuardAreaRespawn  Activate/ObjectDestroyed/DipolInFinished/Stop (respawns on death)
    params VCA_*/VGA_*: Area, Velocity, Mode, Diameter, 4x Priority, WaitingTime, Callback
  vesselgoto.sco -- movement:
    LVG_SimpleGoose/ComplexGoose  fly a path (Goto chain), die-aware
    LVG_MultiPatrol               loop a patrol
    LVG_ComplexGoto               path + timers + sink handling
    params LGU_*: Position, ToDoList, Avoidance, Timer, MemoryPosition, CallBack
  misc.sco -- escorts & specials:
    LM_MilkRunGoto/Patrol   escorted transport run (Vessel_EscortObjectLost handling)
    LM_ComplexGotoAttack    go somewhere and fight
    LM_ProtectedGoto/Patrol LM_FollowProtect  guard another vessel
    LM_Infiltrate           the infiltration mission verb (Infiltrate Begin/Success/
                            Finished callbacks)
    params CGA_*: FightingArea, WatchDistance, Velocity, Mode, 4x Pri, CommandTable
  formation.sco -- group flying:
    LF_Leader / LF_Follower / LF_GotoInFormation[FromTo]
    LF_FormationFunctions provides shapes: Triangle, Line, Row, InverseTriangle,
        XForm, Claw, Cross, Tunnel.  params VGIF_*: Formation, Path, Velocity, Group
  dock.sco: LD_DockMaster / LD_Docking / LV_Docking (station docking choreography;
    ReleaseShip/ClearForDocking/PlaceInDock/Undock states) -- used by sta_*.sco.
  turret.sco: LT_Attack (turret target loop; Body_EnemyArrival/ComplexAttackOver).
  oldone.sco: OO_Goto/Attack/Sink -- the boss "Old One" (uses PhysicalCharacter_*).
  dipol.sco: LD_Jump -- the dipol/afterburner jump-in (Vessel/Vehicle_DipolInFinished).

HOW A MISSION USES ONE (the pattern, from campaign scripts):
    -- in __StartUp, after creating and BindEasy-ing the vessel's thread:
    StoreInnerValue(thread, "VGA_PatrolArea", areaNode)
    StoreInnerValue(thread, "VGA_Velocity", 300)
    StoreInnerValue(thread, "VGA_Diameter", 400)
    ... set the VGA_*Priority target order ...
    GotoState(thread, LVA_GuardArea)        -- enter the behaviour
The vessel now patrols the area and engages intruders by priority, entirely via
library Lua -- no new AI code. To retune campaign AI, edit these inner values or the
library .sco; both are in the PAKs.

WHY THIS IS THE KEY TO MISSION SCRIPTING. A mission is: build the scene (Node_/OSD),
create vessels + threads, StoreInnerValue the tuning, GotoState into a library
behaviour, and wire Trigger_/WayPoint_ events (section 56) to switch behaviours or
fire takes. Everything is data + library Lua. A NEW mission can be assembled almost
entirely from these 27 verbs + the trigger system, without touching the engine.

STILL TODO: decompile LD_DockMaster fully (for the daGama restore -- it drives the
station dock); the exact VGA_Mode enum values; LF formation geometry maths; a
worked "hello-mission" template built from these blocks.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
58. MISSION PROGRESSION -- objectives, the boolean logic net, and victory
    (analysis session 3; campaign 1n1 + glue)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
How a mission states its goal, tracks it, and ends. Measured usage across campaigns:
Game_SetTaskTextKey in 70 script contexts, Game_LoadMTake 37, Game_MissionEnd 34,
SendRadioMessageTake 32, Game_SetWayPoint 30.

MISSION END (the simplest part):
    Game_MissionEnd(GetGameNode(), success)     -- success = 1 win, 0 fail
    Game_EndGame()                              -- ends the whole game (campaign over)
    Game_SetTaskTextKey(GetGameNode(), key, a, b)  -- shows the current objective text
        (key -> a text-table entry; the on-screen "Task"/objective line)
    Game_SetWayPoint(node)                      -- sets the HUD waypoint marker to a node

THE VICTORY CONDITION IS A BOOLEAN LOGIC NETWORK. Missions do NOT poll a flag in a
loop. They build a small graph of LOGIC OBJECTS (named o2, o1700, o1725, ... in the
level script), each a table with:
    .Value            current boolean (True/False globals)
    .GetCalculated()  recompute my value from my inputs
    .StartCalculate / .ReCalculate   propagate a change through the net
    .ChangeTo1 / .ChangeTo0          transition actions (can fire radio takes etc.)
A leaf's value is driven by a GAME EVENT; interior nodes are AND/OR of their inputs;
the ROOT node's ChangeTo1 calls Game_MissionEnd(...,1). Example from 1n1: when the
watched objects report in, o2.GetCalculated() flips, o2.Value = true, and o2's action
proto is  Game_MissionEnd(GetGameNode(), 1)  -- mission won. Interior actions also fire
story beats, e.g. proto 155: SendRadioMessageTake(o1692.Node, o1692.Node, 1129) when a
sub-condition becomes true.

HOW EVENTS FEED THE NET (the bridge):
    SetEngineValue(state, key, value)   [glue proto 67] =
        CallFunction(state.Thread, "SetStateValue_"..key, value)
    i.e. it calls a named setter "SetStateValue_<key>" on the thread. Those setters are
    generated per level (SED_SetTaskTextKey, SetStateValue_Triggered, SetStateValue_
    KineticShield25/50, ...). A behaviour/sensor firing SetEngineValue(state,"Destroyed",
    1) or (state,"Triggered",1) thus pokes a value that a logic-object's GetCalculated
    reads -> the net recomputes -> maybe the root flips -> Game_MissionEnd. This is the
    clean chain: sensor/callback -> SetEngineValue -> logic net -> victory.
    StoreInnerValue(thread,key,v)/GetInnerValue(thread,key) are just thread.CommandTable
    [key] = v / read (glue protos 73/74) -- the per-thread parameter store used both for
    behaviour tuning (section 57) and as logic inputs.

MODIFICATION STACK (how objectives change mid-mission): ApplyModification /
ForceModification / RestoreModification (glue) swap a thread's CommandTable for a new
one ("[MOD] <node>: Applying modification ... Level: <n>"), pushing the old onto HLB
(a history stack). This is how a mission re-tasks a vessel or the player when an
objective completes -- e.g. after "destroy X", modify to "return to base".

THE COMPLETE MISSION SKELETON (everything now known, sections 33-34,56,57,58):
    __InitLayout:  build terrain/structures/atmosphere; create vessels, triggers,
                   waypoints, and logic objects; Game_SetAmbientLight/Fog/etc.
    __StartUp:     Game_LoadMTake(...); set the enemy matrix; bind each vessel thread
                   (BindEasy) and StoreInnerValue its tuning; GotoState into a library
                   behaviour (section 57); bind triggers (section 56); wire logic-object
                   leaves to SetStateValue_ setters; Game_SetTaskTextKey(objective);
                   G_ScriptTimerThread = BindEasy(.../ "/script", C_ScriptTimer);
                   Game_LoadProgress_Leave.
    Runtime:       triggers/behaviour callbacks -> SetEngineValue -> logic net -> when
                   the root is true, Game_MissionEnd(gameNode, 1); ChangeTo actions fire
                   radio takes / SetTaskTextKey updates / modifications along the way.
That is a full AquaNox mission, and every layer of it is editable data + library Lua.

STILL TODO: the logic-object constructor (what builds o2 with GetCalculated/And/Or) --
likely a small level-embedded helper or a lib; a worked minimal mission template;
LD_DockMaster decompile for the daGama dock.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
58. MISSION PROGRESSION -- objectives, the boolean goal graph, and how a mission ends
    (analysis session 3; decompiled 1n1 progression objects + glue)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
The last piece: how a mission tracks objectives and decides win/lose. Usage across
the 54 levels: Game_SetTaskTextKey in ALL 54, Game_MissionEnd in 34 (MP maps end via
frag limits, not this), Game_SetWayPoint 30, SendRadioMessageTake 32.

THE ENGINE-FACING VERBS (ids from the registry, section 49):
    Game_MissionEnd(int32 success)   11036   1 = won, 0 = lost. Called as
        Game_MissionEnd(GetGameNode(), 1). Ends the mission -> debrief screen.
    Game_SetTaskTextKey(int32 key, float, float)  11041   sets the on-screen OBJECTIVE
        text (an mtake/task key, section 42); the two floats are display params.
        Every level sets task text; this is the "current objective" line.
    Game_SetWayPoint(node)   11039   points the HUD nav marker at a node.
    Game_TextMessage(int8, string)   11034   a raw HUD line (no take lookup).
    Game_EndGame()   11030   ends the whole game (final mission / credits).

THE GOAL GRAPH (the clever bit). Missions do NOT hard-code "if X and Y then win".
The compiler emits a network of BOOLEAN NODES as generic script objects, each with:
    GetCalculated()  -> evaluates a big AND/OR expression over OTHER nodes' .Value
                        (a 148-instruction chain of True/False comparisons + jumps)
    ChangeTo1()      -> self.Value = GetCalculated()      (recompute my value)
    StartCalculate() -> SetStateValue(GetCalculated())    (recompute + propagate up)
    SetStateValue()  -> at the ROOT node this is literally Game_MissionEnd(1)
So the objectives form a dependency tree: leaf conditions feed intermediate AND/OR
nodes feed the root; when the root computes True, the mission ends in success. A
"mission failed" branch is a parallel graph whose root calls Game_MissionEnd(0).

HOW A LEAF IS TRIPPED (closes the loop with sections 34/56/57):
    A vessel/trigger's state has a field like Triggered or Destroyed. The sensor/
    death callback does  SetEngineValue(thread, "Destroyed", 1).
    glue.SetEngineValue(thread, name, val)  ==  CallFunction(thread.Thread,
        "SetStateValue_"..name, val)   -- calls the dynamically-named method
    That method sets the leaf node's .Value and calls StartCalculate(), which
    recomputes up the tree. If the root becomes True -> Game_MissionEnd.
So the chain is: enemy dies / player enters trigger -> callback SetEngineValue ->
SetStateValue_<name> -> leaf.Value = 1 -> propagate -> root -> Game_MissionEnd.
This is why our sp_1n1 wave mod set "Destroyed" on the toilers via SetEngineValue --
that IS the objective-completion signal (section 6b-1).

MISSION SKELETON (the complete recipe, all data + library Lua):
  __InitLayout: build scene; create vessels + triggers; place waypoints.
  __StartUp:
    Game_LoadMTake(...) ; Game_LoadTextTable(...)          -- load text/voice
    Game_SetTaskTextKey(objKey, ...)                       -- show first objective
    for each vessel: BindEasy -> thread; StoreInnerValue tuning;
        GotoState(thread, LVA_GuardArea / LM_Infiltrate / ...)  -- give it behaviour (s.57)
    for each trigger: BindEasy a state whose EnterPresence does SetEngineValue(...)  (s.56)
    build the goal graph: leaf nodes for each objective flag, AND/OR nodes, a root
        whose SetStateValue = Game_MissionEnd(1); a fail root = Game_MissionEnd(0)
    G_ScriptTimerThread = BindEasy(Node_Find("/"), "/script", C_ScriptTimer)  -- the tick
  Runtime: sensors/deaths -> SetEngineValue -> graph recompute -> MissionEnd.

FOR A SIMPLE CUSTOM MISSION you don't need the full graph: a single objective can be
"toiler destroyed -> Game_MissionEnd(1)" directly in the death callback, exactly as the
wave mod does. The graph only earns its keep for multi-condition objectives.

STILL TODO: the exact Game_SetTaskTextKey float params; the "mission failed" variants
(timeout, player death, escort lost); Game_SetWayPoint HUD-marker chaining; a written
end-to-end hello-mission .sco assembled from sections 56/57/58.
(A commented mission TEMPLATE tying sections 34/56/57/58 together as readable pseudo-
Lua was written to aquanox_mission_vorlage.md -- scene structure, __InitLayout/__StartUp
skeleton, behaviour binding, trigger reaction, objective completion, snoop debugging,
and the 4 common pitfalls.)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
59. HOW CAMPAIGN MISSIONS ARE REALLY SCRIPTED -- surveyed ALL 30 (corrects an assumption)
    (analysis session 3; parsed every map/*/script/*.sco)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
IMPORTANT CORRECTION to section 57's mental picture. I had only deep-read a few levels
before. Parsing ALL 70 map scripts (30 campaign + MP + instant + station) shows campaign
missions do NOT call GotoState(thread, LVA_GuardArea) directly. That direct form is how
YOU would use the library from a hand-written script; the SHIPPED missions use a
DIFFERENT, compiler-generated layer on top.

WHAT EVERY CAMPAIGN OBJECT ACTUALLY HAS. Each of the ~1054 vessels/objects across the 30
missions gets its own generated STATE MACHINE table S_o<NNNN> (1h1 alone has 209). The
table's keys are a fixed vocabulary:
    Start                      -- entry: sets self.CodeIndex=0 and calls Code0
    Code0, Code1, ... CodeN    -- the ordered SCRIPTED STEPS for this object
    CheckCondition             -- returns whether the current CodeIndex step is allowed
    ProcesseStateChange        -- if CheckCondition passes: StopAllActiveBehaviours_AndCall
                                  ("Code"..CodeIndex) -- i.e. run the next step
    SetStateValue_<COND>       -- condition hooks the engine/sensors fire, e.g.
        _Destroyed, _Triggered, _KineticShield25/50/75, _FriendlyFire1/2/3
So a mission object is a linear SCRIPT of Code steps, advanced by conditions. Loaded
libraries (VesselAttackLib = LoadLib("VesselAttack"), etc.) are pulled in, and a Code
step is what calls into them (a Code block does the StoreInnerValue + GotoState into
LVA_/LVG_/... under the hood). The whole thing is driven by CallFunction (648 calls in
1h1) routing named methods, and StopAllActiveBehaviours_AndCall to switch steps cleanly.

THE CONDITION SYSTEM (this is the real objective/trigger glue).
  * A sensor/death/shield event calls SetEngineValue(thread, "<COND>", val), which
    (glue, section 58) becomes CallFunction(thread.Thread, "SetStateValue_<COND>", val).
  * The object's SetStateValue_<COND> forwards to a shared condition object o<NNNN>'s
    SetStateValue -- the boolean goal-graph node (section 58).
  * StandardconditionS the engine provides for free: Destroyed (ship died),
    Triggered (a NOD_Trigger fired), KineticShield25/50/75 (hull thresholds),
    FriendlyFire1/2/3 (player shot allies -- escalating). These are the pre-wired hooks
    a mission scripts against without any custom sensor code.

SURVEY OF ALL 30 CAMPAIGN MISSIONS (aquanox1_mission_scripts.json has per-level detail):
  total 1054 vessels, 77 triggers, 34,614 script protos. ALL 30 use Game_MissionEnd,
  Game_SetTaskTextKey and SendRadioMessageTake. 20/30 use NOD_Trigger volumes; the other
  10 drive purely off Destroyed/arrival conditions. Size ranges from 1n1 (187 protos,
  7 ships -- the tutorial-scale side mission) to 6h2 (3426 protos, 131 ships -- the
  climactic battle). WayPoints appear in all with the full 6-takekey idiom.

WHAT THIS MEANS FOR SCRIPTING YOUR OWN MISSION. Two valid styles:
  (1) LIBRARY-DIRECT (simplest, what our wave mod + section-57 template use): create a
      thread, StoreInnerValue, GotoState into a library behaviour, and put
      Game_MissionEnd in a Body_ObjectDestroyed callback. Great for small missions.
  (2) CAMPAIGN-STYLE (what the game does): give each object a S_o table with Start +
      Code0..N steps + CheckCondition + ProcesseStateChange, and SetStateValue_<COND>
      hooks; advance the object through its Code steps as conditions fire. This scales to
      scripted, multi-stage set-pieces (a transport that flies in, docks, is ambushed,
      you must defend it -> each phase is a Code step gated by a condition).
Both compile to the same primitives; style (1) is a hand-friendly subset of (2).

RADIO / FUNKSPRUCH -- the definitive picture (ties sections 42/56/58):
  Missions fire takes THREE ways, all seen across the 30:
    a) WayPoint_SetTakeKey_Enter/Inside/Leave/Outside/Activate(wp, key) -- location-based,
       engine-timed, the SAFEST for an intro line (fires from a live callback).
    b) SendRadioMessageTake(sender, receiver, key) from inside a Code step or a
       condition hook (e.g. right after StartCalculate, or in EnterPresence).
    c) DelayedFunction(seconds, thread, "CodeK") to speak a line after a beat -- but ONLY
       after G_ScriptTimerThread is bound (section 35).
  The one place takes DON'T reliably fire is a bare __StartUp call before the sim/HUD is
  live (the intro-radio problem) -- use (a) or a bound-thread condition instead.

STILL TODO: decompile one full campaign Code0..N chain end-to-end (e.g. a transport
escort) as a worked example; the StopAllActiveBehaviours_AndCall internals; enumerate
every SetStateValue_<COND> the engine can raise.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
60. THE OTHER 40 SCRIPTS -- stations, instant fights, MP maps (+ CUT MAP "Vulcano")
    (analysis session 3; parsed all 70 map scripts, not just the campaign)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Section 59 covered the 30 campaign missions. Here are the other 40 -- 16 stations,
4 instant fights, 9 MP maps (+ our own sp_*). All use the SAME generated S_o state
machine model (section 59), which confirms one editor produced every level type.

STATIONS (16 scripts, all exterior/backdrop scenes -- section 45):
    43..320 protos, 3..14 ambient vessels, 17..95 S_o machines, NO Game_MissionEnd,
    NO radio takes, and all reference the docking apparatus. sta_magellan is the
    richest (320 protos, 8 triggers, 95 machines); sta_entropointii the leanest (43).
    Triggers appear only in sta_entropoint/ix/vi (2 each) and sta_magellan (8) -- these
    are the approach/dock-permission zones.

INSTANT FIGHTS (4): they ARE full missions, not arenas:
    dogfight       501 protos,  1 vessel, 15 triggers, 54 machines, MissionEnd
    inst_asylum    822 protos, 43 vessels,  0 triggers, 104 machines, MissionEnd
    inst_fightclub 362 protos,  5 vessels,  1 trigger,  60 machines, MissionEnd + radio
    inst_succubus  372 protos,  1 vessel,   1 trigger,  49 machines, MissionEnd + radio
    So dogfight is a 15-trigger tutorial-style course ("follow the NAV points",
    "destroy the relay" -- matching its 3 takes 9000-9002, section 42), and inst_asylum
    is a 43-ship brawl. All end via Game_MissionEnd -> they are the closest template
    for a custom standalone mission.

MULTIPLAYER (9): tiny scripts (11..75 protos), ZERO vessels, no MissionEnd (frag/flag
    limits end them), 8..67 S_o machines that mostly manage item boxes and spawns.
    dm_sightless is the smallest script in the game (11 protos).

*** ROADMAP HIT: THE CUT MAP mp_07 "Vulcano" IS COMPLETE AND SHIPPING ***
  map/mp_07_ctf/ contains 117 files -- MORE than the working Liberty map (100):
      fog/fog.fog (1 MB) + fog.des + fog.tga        (its own atmosphere)
      lmsh/ 80 .msb meshes incl. atl_brainfire_blue/_red with all child parts
      23 .dds textures, 7 .tga (terrain grass_01/stone_01), 3 .des
      script/ctf_vulcano.sco   (81,588 B, 70 protos)  <- the real level
      script/cft_vulcano.sco   (29,339 B, 10 protos)  <- an earlier/typo'd variant
  ctf_vulcano.sco builds: 130 nod_itembox, 64 nod_generic, 38 particle emitters,
  18 positions, 15 directories, 8 fungus, and the CTF furniture team_blue/red bases +
  team_*_respawn_1..8. Terrain_LoadTerrain + Game_SetFog + Game_SetTerrainDepth.
  It is a finished, playable CTF map.
  WHY IT NEVER APPEARS: dat/menu/maplist~.des (cooked DES) lists numofmaps = 8 with
  map0..map7 = mp_05_ctf, mp_06_dm, mp_08, mp_09, mp_10, mp_11, mp_12, mp_13.
  mp_07 IS SIMPLY NOT IN THE LIST.
  RESTORE (data only): rebuild maplist~.des with numofmaps = 9 and one more entry
      [map8] { path = "map\mp_07_ctf\script\ctf_vulcano" , gametype = "ctf" }
  (descook/recook it; the file is only 793 bytes.) Nothing else is missing.
  This is roadmap item 5 and it is even easier than the daGama station restore.

WHAT THIS SURVEY ADDS FOR SCRIPTING: instant fights are the best worked examples of a
SELF-CONTAINED mission (scene + a few ships + triggers + MissionEnd + takes) without
campaign progression baggage. dogfight in particular is a compact 501-proto script that
does objectives via triggers and NAV waypoints -- the ideal thing to read before writing
your own.
